========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
They sent me here. My lieutenant, mostly. It was at the station... I kept moving the airway bags into a pattern because I just knew it was right, you know? And I was repeating the dispatch numbers out loud. Dispatch numbers are weird now, they upgraded the CAD system last month and the screens in the rigs are way too bright. Gives you a headache if you stare at it on the night shift. I usually work nights, it's quieter, except when the bars close down on 5th street and everyone floods the diner... 

Anyway, I wouldn't step away from the bags. The lieutenant told me to stop, but I couldn't. It felt like the whole bay would go bad if I broke the sequence. The bay is huge, fits four rigs, but the concrete is always cracking near the center drain. We put in a work order for that drain like six months ago and the city never sent anyone to fix it. Just like the suspension on truck 42... but yeah, I couldn't break the sequence.

I don't really... I don't know what exactly would happen. Just bad. I didn't have a specific picture in my head or anything, I just knew I couldn't stop. It's just a feeling. Like if you don't secure the oxygen tanks right in the back of the rig. You ever hear an O2 tank hit the metal floor? It's so loud. The valves on those things are brass. 

Brass is getting really expensive lately, actually. I was at the hardware store on Sunday looking for a new fitting for my garden hose, and they had all the brass stuff locked behind this glass case. You have to press a little red button to get an employee to come open it. Took the guy twenty minutes to show up. He was wearing this bright orange vest... exact same color as the traffic cones we use. We lose so many cones on the interstate because people just drive right over them. They don't even slow down. The reflective tape peels off them after a week anyway...

No, no specific thought. I wasn't... there was no image or anything like that. My head was just kind of empty, honestly. I just had my hands on the bags. The blue ones. They use this really stiff nylon for the blue ones now, you know? The zippers always catch on the corners if you pull them too fast. We had a guy, a rookie named Miller, who ripped the zipper right off the pediatric bag last month. Just yanked it. 

You can't just yank things, you have to be smooth. Like when you're driving the rig, you can't slam on the brakes or you throw everyone in the back against the bulkhead. The bulkhead in our new rig is padded, which is nice, but it smells like... I don't know, industrial glue. It's like that new car smell but way more chemical. My dad used to work at a chemical plant down by the river. They made fertilizer, I think. The river always smelled terrible in the summer when the water levels dropped. We used to go fishing down there anyway, catching catfish. Catfish will eat basically anything, they just scrape the bottom...

No, I don't think so. I mean, reality is just what's happening, right? Sometimes things just happen and you're in the middle of it. Like when we get a call for a multi-vehicle pileup on I-95. That feels pretty real. You get there and there's glass everywhere. Safety glass, you know, it shatters into those little cubes so it doesn't slice you up as bad as regular glass. They temper it. 

I actually tried to temper some steel in my garage once. I watched a bunch of videos on it. You have to heat it up until it's non-magnetic, like a cherry red color, and then quench it in oil. I used peanut oil because it has a higher smoke point, but it still smoked up the whole garage. My neighbor came over because he thought my house was on fire. He's this retired postal worker, always wearing those blue shorts they issue them, even in November. November is when the leaves finally drop off the oak tree in my front yard. I have to rake them every weekend or they kill the grass underneath. I tried that weed-and-feed stuff last spring, but I think I put too much down because it burned a huge yellow patch right near the driveway...

They use this really stiff nylon for the blue ones now, you know? The zippers always catch on the corners if you pull them too fast. We had a guy, a rookie named Miller, who ripped the zipper right off the pediatric bag last month. Just yanked it. \n\nYou can't just yank things, you have to be smooth. Like when you're driving the rig, you can't slam on the brakes or you throw everyone in the back against the bulkhead. The bulkhead in our new rig is padded, which is nice, but it smells like... I don't know, industrial glue. It's like that new car smell but way more chemical. My dad used to work at a chemical plant down by the river. They made fertilizer, I think. The river always smelled terrible in the summer when the water levels dropped. We used to go fishing down there anyway, catching catfish. Catfish will eat basically anything, they just scrape the bottom...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts were disconnected from reality or that things around you didn’t feel completely real?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, reality is just what's happening, right? Sometimes things just happen and you're in the middle of it. Like when we get a call for a multi-vehicle pileup on I-95. That feels pretty real. You get there and there's glass everywhere. Safety glass, you know, it shatters into those little cubes so it doesn't slice you up as bad as regular glass. They temper it. \n\nI actually tried to temper some steel in my garage once. I watched a bunch of videos on it. You have to heat it up until it's non-magnetic, like a cherry red color, and then quench it in oil. I used peanut oil because it has a higher smoke point, but it still smoked up the whole garage. My neighbor came over because he thought my house was on fire. He's this retired postal worker, always wearing those blue shorts they issue them, even in November. November is when the leaves finally drop off the oak tree in my front yard. I have to rake them every weekend or they kill the grass underneath. I tried that weed-and-feed stuff last spring, but I think I put too much down because it burned a huge yellow patch right near the driveway...\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had any beliefs or ideas that other people around you didn't share, even if they tried to tell you otherwise?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / emergency_medical_technician / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I know the injury review people and some supervisors have been watching me through the station cameras and changing my triage notes to make me look unsafe, even when they tell me it's just routine paperwork.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean... the supervisors tell me it's just routine paperwork, but I know what's going on. The injury review people and some of the supervisors, they've been watching me through the station cameras. They go in and change my triage notes to make me look unsafe. They keep saying they aren't, that it's just normal audits, but I know they are. 

They have those new dome cameras in the bays now. They installed them back in March, I think. Said it was for security because someone stole a monitor out of an unlocked rig in district four. District four is mostly downtown, lots of narrow streets. It's a nightmare trying to back the rig up down there, especially when people park on both sides of the street. You have to rely on your partner to spot you, and if they aren't paying attention, you're taking off a mirror. The mirrors on the new F-series chassis are massive. They stick out like two feet. The glass on them has that little convex bubble in the corner so you can see your blind spot, but it distorts everything. It makes the cars look way farther away than they actually are. I almost backed into a fire hydrant once because of that distortion. Hydrants are supposed to be bright yellow or red, but the city painted some of them this weird dark green to blend in with the landscaping. It makes no sense. If there's a fire, you want to find the water fast, not go hunting for it in the bushes...

Catfish will eat basically anything, they just scrape the bottom...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts were disconnected from reality or that things around you didn’t feel completely real?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, reality is just what's happening, right? Sometimes things just happen and you're in the middle of it. Like when we get a call for a multi-vehicle pileup on I-95. That feels pretty real. You get there and there's glass everywhere. Safety glass, you know, it shatters into those little cubes so it doesn't slice you up as bad as regular glass. They temper it. \n\nI actually tried to temper some steel in my garage once. I watched a bunch of videos on it. You have to heat it up until it's non-magnetic, like a cherry red color, and then quench it in oil. I used peanut oil because it has a higher smoke point, but it still smoked up the whole garage. My neighbor came over because he thought my house was on fire. He's this retired postal worker, always wearing those blue shorts they issue them, even in November. November is when the leaves finally drop off the oak tree in my front yard. I have to rake them every weekend or they kill the grass underneath. I tried that weed-and-feed stuff last spring, but I think I put too much down because it burned a huge yellow patch right near the driveway...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or ideas that other people around you didn't share, even if they tried to tell you otherwise?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... the supervisors tell me it's just routine paperwork, but I know what's going on. The injury review people and some of the supervisors, they've been watching me through the station cameras. They go in and change my triage notes to make me look unsafe. They keep saying they aren't, that it's just normal audits, but I know they are. \n\nThey have those new dome cameras in the bays now. They installed them back in March, I think. Said it was for security because someone stole a monitor out of an unlocked rig in district four. District four is mostly downtown, lots of narrow streets. It's a nightmare trying to back the rig up down there, especially when people park on both sides of the street. You have to rely on your partner to spot you, and if they aren't paying attention, you're taking off a mirror. The mirrors on the new F-series chassis are massive. They stick out like two feet. The glass on them has that little convex bubble in the corner so you can see your blind spot, but it distorts everything. It makes the cars look way farther away than they actually are. I almost backed into a fire hydrant once because of that distortion. Hydrants are supposed to be bright yellow or red, but the city painted some of them this weird dark green to blend in with the landscaping. It makes no sense. If there's a fire, you want to find the water fast, not go hunting for it in the bushes...\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you tell me more about how you know they are changing your triage notes? Have you seen them do this, or do you believe they are doing it based on something else?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / emergency_medical_technician / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I know the injury review people and some supervisors have been watching me through the station cameras and changing my triage notes to make me look unsafe, even when they tell me it's just routine paperwork.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I just... I haven't caught them physically typing at my desk, if that's what you mean. But I know they're doing it. They watch me on the cameras, and they go in and change my triage notes to make me look unsafe. They keep telling me, "Oh, it's just routine paperwork, it's a standard audit," but I know what they're up to. 

The software we use for the reports is this really old system, anyway. It takes forever to boot up on the Toughbooks. We have these Panasonic Toughbooks in the rigs, and they're supposed to be indestructible, right? Like, you can drop them right on the pavement. I actually saw a guy drop one once right in the middle of an intersection. It was raining, too. The rain here gets so heavy in the spring, it just washes all the oil up from the asphalt and the roads get super slick. You really have to watch your following distance when it's like that. I always leave at least four car lengths, especially if I'm driving my personal car. It's a Honda, older model, but the transmission is starting to slip a little bit when it shifts from second to third. I probably need to check the transmission fluid, but you have to check it when the engine is warm, not cold. A lot of people don't know that. They check it cold and get a false reading on the dipstick, and then they overfill it. Overfilling is almost as bad as running it dry because it foams up...

But I know they're doing it. They watch me on the cameras, and they go in and change my triage notes to make me look unsafe. They keep telling me, \"Oh, it's just routine paperwork, it's a standard audit,\" but I know what they're up to. \n\nThe software we use for the reports is this really old system, anyway. It takes forever to boot up on the Toughbooks. We have these Panasonic Toughbooks in the rigs, and they're supposed to be indestructible, right? Like, you can drop them right on the pavement. I actually saw a guy drop one once right in the middle of an intersection. It was raining, too. The rain here gets so heavy in the spring, it just washes all the oil up from the asphalt and the roads get super slick. You really have to watch your following distance when it's like that. I always leave at least four car lengths, especially if I'm driving my personal car. It's a Honda, older model, but the transmission is starting to slip a little bit when it shifts from second to third. I probably need to check the transmission fluid, but you have to check it when the engine is warm, not cold. A lot of people don't know that. They check it cold and get a false reading on the dipstick, and then they overfill it. Overfilling is almost as bad as running it dry because it foams up...\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel like someone is putting thoughts into your mind or that events or objects around you have special meanings specifically for you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. They temper it. \n\nI actually tried to temper some steel in my garage once. I watched a bunch of videos on it. You have to heat it up until it's non-magnetic, like a cherry red color, and then quench it in oil. I used peanut oil because it has a higher smoke point, but it still smoked up the whole garage. My neighbor came over because he thought my house was on fire. He's this retired postal worker, always wearing those blue shorts they issue them, even in November. November is when the leaves finally drop off the oak tree in my front yard. I have to rake them every weekend or they kill the grass underneath. I tried that weed-and-feed stuff last spring, but I think I put too much down because it burned a huge yellow patch right near the driveway...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or ideas that other people around you didn't share, even if they tried to tell you otherwise?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... the supervisors tell me it's just routine paperwork, but I know what's going on. They keep saying they aren't, that it's just normal audits, but I know they are. \n\nThey have those new dome cameras in the bays now. They installed them back in March, I think. Said it was for security because someone stole a monitor out of an unlocked rig in district four. District four is mostly downtown, lots of narrow streets. It's a nightmare trying to back the rig up down there, especially when people park on both sides of the street. You have to rely on your partner to spot you, and if they aren't paying attention, you're taking off a mirror. The mirrors on the new F-series chassis are massive. They stick out like two feet. The glass on them has that little convex bubble in the corner so you can see your blind spot, but it distorts everything. It makes the cars look way farther away than they actually are. I actually saw a guy drop one once right in the middle of an intersection. It was raining, too. The rain here gets so heavy in the spring, it just washes all the oil up from the asphalt and the roads get super slick. You really have to watch your following distance when it's like that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no one's putting thoughts in my head. That sounds like something out of a sci-fi movie, you know? Like those old black-and-white ones where they wear the tinfoil hats. I actually watched this documentary about how aluminum foil used to be made of tin, but they switched to aluminum after World War II because it was cheaper. Everything got cheaper after the war. Mass production, plastics, all that. We use so much plastic on the rig now. The IV tubing, the flushes, the sterile packaging. It all just gets thrown in the red biohazard bags. Those bags are incredibly thick, though, so they don't puncture. You don't want a needle sticking through and getting you. We have these sharps shuttles, they're little yellow plastic boxes you can carry in your pocket. I always keep mine in my left cargo pocket. The uniform pants have these great pockets with Velcro closures, but the Velcro wears out after a few months of washing them. I try to wash them on the gentle cycle, but the blood and the grease from the road, you really have to use heavy-duty detergent to get it out. I buy the big tubs of powder detergent at Costco. You can get like a hundred loads out of one tub, and it's way cheaper than the liquid stuff. Plus, the liquid stuff comes in those heavy plastic jugs that are terrible for the environment. I try to recycle them, but our city's recycling program is a mess. They only pick up every other week, and half the time they leave the bins sitting out in the middle of the street...

The injury review people and some of the supervisors, they've been watching me through the station cameras. They go in and change my triage notes to make me look unsafe. They keep saying they aren't, that it's just normal audits, but I know they are. \n\nThey have those new dome cameras in the bays now. They installed them back in March, I think. Said it was for security because someone stole a monitor out of an unlocked rig in district four. District four is mostly downtown, lots of narrow streets. It's a nightmare trying to back the rig up down there, especially when people park on both sides of the street. You have to rely on your partner to spot you, and if they aren't paying attention, you're taking off a mirror. The mirrors on the new F-series chassis are massive. They stick out like two feet. The glass on them has that little convex bubble in the corner so you can see your blind spot, but it distorts everything. It makes the cars look way farther away than they actually are. I almost backed into a fire hydrant once because of that distortion. Hydrants are supposed to be bright yellow or red, but the city painted some of them this weird dark green to blend in with the landscaping. It makes no sense. If there's a fire, you want to find the water fast, not go hunting for it in the bushes...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you know they are changing your triage notes? Have you seen them do this, or do you believe they are doing it based on something else?\n\nPatient: I just... I haven't caught them physically typing at my desk, if that's what you mean. But I know they're doing it. They watch me on the cameras, and they go in and change my triage notes to make me look unsafe. They keep telling me, \"Oh, it's just routine paperwork, it's a standard audit,\" but I know what they're up to. \n\nThe software we use for the reports is this really old system, anyway. It takes forever to boot up on the Toughbooks. We have these Panasonic Toughbooks in the rigs, and they're supposed to be indestructible, right? Like, you can drop them right on the pavement. I actually saw a guy drop one once right in the middle of an intersection. It was raining, too. The rain here gets so heavy in the spring, it just washes all the oil up from the asphalt and the roads get super slick. You really have to watch your following distance when it's like that. I always leave at least four car lengths, especially if I'm driving my personal car. It's a Honda, older model, but the transmission is starting to slip a little bit when it shifts from second to third. I probably need to check the transmission fluid, but you have to check it when the engine is warm, not cold. A lot of people don't know that. They check it cold and get a false reading on the dipstick, and then they overfill it. Overfilling is almost as bad as running it dry because it foams up...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like someone is putting thoughts into your mind or that events or objects around you have special meanings specifically for you?\n\nPatient: No, no one's putting thoughts in my head. That sounds like something out of a sci-fi movie, you know? Like those old black-and-white ones where they wear the tinfoil hats. I actually watched this documentary about how aluminum foil used to be made of tin, but they switched to aluminum after World War II because it was cheaper. Everything got cheaper after the war. Mass production, plastics, all that. We use so much plastic on the rig now. The IV tubing, the flushes, the sterile packaging. It all just gets thrown in the red biohazard bags. Those bags are incredibly thick, though, so they don't puncture. You don't want a needle sticking through and getting you. We have these sharps shuttles, they're little yellow plastic boxes you can carry in your pocket. I always keep mine in my left cargo pocket. The uniform pants have these great pockets with Velcro closures, but the Velcro wears out after a few months of washing them. I try to wash them on the gentle cycle, but the blood and the grease from the road, you really have to use heavy-duty detergent to get it out. I buy the big tubs of powder detergent at Costco. You can get like a hundred loads out of one tub, and it's way cheaper than the liquid stuff. Plus, the liquid stuff comes in those heavy plastic jugs that are terrible for the environment. I try to recycle them, but our city's recycling program is a mess. They only pick up every other week, and half the time they leave the bins sitting out in the middle of the street...\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt that certain events or objects in your environment were specifically meant for you, like they were sending you a message or had a hidden meaning just for you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't think so. Like secret messages? No, nothing like that. I mean, the only messages I get are the dispatch tones on the pager, and those are definitely not secret. They're so loud. The tones we use are these high-pitched dual-frequency things, and they'll wake you out of a dead sleep at 3 AM. It's terrible for your heart rate, honestly. They did a study on first responders and found our resting heart rates spike like crazy when the tones drop. It's all that adrenaline. Adrenaline is a hormone, right? Produced by the adrenal glands, which sit right on top of your kidneys. Kidneys are fascinating, they filter all your blood. People who need dialysis have to go to the clinic like three times a week because their kidneys don't work. We transport a lot of dialysis patients. Some of them are really nice, but some get so cranky. I guess I'd be cranky too if I had to sit in a chair for four hours hooked up to a machine. The machines are so loud, they just beep constantly. I hate things that beep. My microwave at home beeps five times when it's done, and it won't stop until you open the door. It's a GE microwave, stainless steel. I got it on sale at Home Depot last year when they were doing their Memorial Day appliance event. They always have good sales on Memorial Day, but the parking lot is a total nightmare...

We had a guy, a rookie named Miller, who ripped the zipper right off the pediatric bag last month. Just yanked it. \n\nYou can't just yank things, you have to be smooth. They temper it. \n\nI actually tried to temper some steel in my garage once. I watched a bunch of videos on it. You have to heat it up until it's non-magnetic, like a cherry red color, and then quench it in oil. I used peanut oil because it has a higher smoke point, but it still smoked up the whole garage. It's a nightmare trying to back the rig up down there, especially when people park on both sides of the street. You have to rely on your partner to spot you, and if they aren't paying attention, you're taking off a mirror. The mirrors on the new F-series chassis are massive. They stick out like two feet. The glass on them has that little convex bubble in the corner so you can see your blind spot, but it distorts everything. It makes the cars look way farther away than they actually are. I almost backed into a fire hydrant once because of that distortion. Hydrants are supposed to be bright yellow or red, but the city painted some of them this weird dark green to blend in with the landscaping. It makes no sense. If there's a fire, you want to find the water fast, not go hunting for it in the bushes...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you know they are changing your triage notes? They keep telling me, \"Oh, it's just routine paperwork, it's a standard audit,\" but I know what they're up to. \n\nThe software we use for the reports is this really old system, anyway. It takes forever to boot up on the Toughbooks. We have these Panasonic Toughbooks in the rigs, and they're supposed to be indestructible, right? Like, you can drop them right on the pavement. I actually saw a guy drop one once right in the middle of an intersection. It was raining, too. The rain here gets so heavy in the spring, it just washes all the oil up from the asphalt and the roads get super slick. You really have to watch your following distance when it's like that. I always leave at least four car lengths, especially if I'm driving my personal car. It's a Honda, older model, but the transmission is starting to slip a little bit when it shifts from second to third. I probably need to check the transmission fluid, but you have to check it when the engine is warm, not cold. A lot of people don't know that. They check it cold and get a false reading on the dipstick, and then they overfill it. Overfilling is almost as bad as running it dry because it foams up...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like someone is putting thoughts into your mind or that events or objects around you have special meanings specifically for you?\n\nPatient: No, no one's putting thoughts in my head. I actually watched this documentary about how aluminum foil used to be made of tin, but they switched to aluminum after World War II because it was cheaper. Everything got cheaper after the war. Mass production, plastics, all that. We use so much plastic on the rig now. The IV tubing, the flushes, the sterile packaging. It all just gets thrown in the red biohazard bags. Those bags are incredibly thick, though, so they don't puncture. You don't want a needle sticking through and getting you. The uniform pants have these great pockets with Velcro closures, but the Velcro wears out after a few months of washing them. You can get like a hundred loads out of one tub, and it's way cheaper than the liquid stuff. Plus, the liquid stuff comes in those heavy plastic jugs that are terrible for the environment. I try to recycle them, but our city's recycling program is a mess. It's definitely a plan. The injury review people and some of the supervisors, they're the ones watching me through the station cameras. They're changing my triage notes on purpose to make me look unsafe, even though they keep telling me it's just routine paperwork. Paperwork is such a hassle anyway. The humidity here in the summer is just brutal, it feels like you're breathing underwater. I went to Florida once for vacation, to Orlando, and it was even worse there. We went to Disney World, but the lines were so long we barely got to ride anything. Space Mountain was closed for maintenance the whole time we were there, which was a huge disappointment because I love roller coasters. The physics of roller coasters are actually really cool, how they use potential energy at the top of the hill and convert it to kinetic energy...

The lieutenant told me to stop, but I couldn't. It felt like the whole bay would go bad if I broke the sequence. The bay is huge, fits four rigs, but the concrete is always cracking near the center drain. We put in a work order for that drain like six months ago and the city never sent anyone to fix it. Just like the suspension on truck 42... but yeah, I couldn't break the sequence.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about why you felt you couldn't break the sequence with the airway bags? Did you believe something specific would happen if you did?\n\nPatient: I don't really... I don't know what exactly would happen. Just bad. I didn't have a specific picture in my head or anything, I just knew I couldn't stop. It's just a feeling. Like if you don't secure the oxygen tanks right in the back of the rig. You ever hear an O2 tank hit the metal floor? It's so loud. The valves on those things are brass. \n\nBrass is getting really expensive lately, actually. I was at the hardware store on Sunday looking for a new fitting for my garden hose, and they had all the brass stuff locked behind this glass case. You have to press a little red button to get an employee to come open it. Took the guy twenty minutes to show up. He was wearing this bright orange vest... exact same color as the traffic cones we use. We lose so many cones on the interstate because people just drive right over them. They don't even slow down. The reflective tape peels off them after a week anyway...\n\nDoctor: When you were arranging the airway bags, did you have any particular thought, urge, or image that kept coming into your mind that made you feel like you had to keep doing it?\n\nPatient: No, no specific thought. I wasn't... there was no image or anything like that. My head was just kind of empty, honestly. I just had my hands on the bags. The blue ones. They use this really stiff nylon for the blue ones now, you know? The zippers always catch on the corners if you pull them too fast. We had a guy, a rookie named Miller, who ripped the zipper right off the pediatric bag last month. Just yanked it. \n\nYou can't just yank things, you have to be smooth. Like when you're driving the rig, you can't slam on the brakes or you throw everyone in the back against the bulkhead. The bulkhead in our new rig is padded, which is nice, but it smells like... I don't know, industrial glue. It's like that new car smell but way more chemical. My dad used to work at a chemical plant down by the river. They made fertilizer, I think. The river always smelled terrible in the summer when the water levels dropped. We used to go fishing down there anyway, catching catfish. Catfish will eat basically anything, they just scrape the bottom...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts were disconnected from reality or that things around you didn’t feel completely real?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, reality is just what's happening, right? Sometimes things just happen and you're in the middle of it. Like when we get a call for a multi-vehicle pileup on I-95. That feels pretty real. You get there and there's glass everywhere. Safety glass, you know, it shatters into those little cubes so it doesn't slice you up as bad as regular glass. They temper it. \n\nI actually tried to temper some steel in my garage once. I watched a bunch of videos on it. You have to heat it up until it's non-magnetic, like a cherry red color, and then quench it in oil. I used peanut oil because it has a higher smoke point, but it still smoked up the whole garage. My neighbor came over because he thought my house was on fire. He's this retired postal worker, always wearing those blue shorts they issue them, even in November. November is when the leaves finally drop off the oak tree in my front yard. I have to rake them every weekend or they kill the grass underneath. I tried that weed-and-feed stuff last spring, but I think I put too much down because it burned a huge yellow patch right near the driveway...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or ideas that other people around you didn't share, even if they tried to tell you otherwise?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... the supervisors tell me it's just routine paperwork, but I know what's going on. The injury review people and some of the supervisors, they've been watching me through the station cameras. They go in and change my triage notes to make me look unsafe. They keep saying they aren't, that it's just normal audits, but I know they are. \n\nThey have those new dome cameras in the bays now. They installed them back in March, I think. Said it was for security because someone stole a monitor out of an unlocked rig in district four. District four is mostly downtown, lots of narrow streets. It's a nightmare trying to back the rig up down there, especially when people park on both sides of the street. You have to rely on your partner to spot you, and if they aren't paying attention, you're taking off a mirror. The mirrors on the new F-series chassis are massive. They stick out like two feet. The glass on them has that little convex bubble in the corner so you can see your blind spot, but it distorts everything. It makes the cars look way farther away than they actually are. I almost backed into a fire hydrant once because of that distortion. Hydrants are supposed to be bright yellow or red, but the city painted some of them this weird dark green to blend in with the landscaping. It makes no sense. If there's a fire, you want to find the water fast, not go hunting for it in the bushes...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you know they are changing your triage notes? Have you seen them do this, or do you believe they are doing it based on something else?\n\nPatient: I just... I haven't caught them physically typing at my desk, if that's what you mean. But I know they're doing it. They watch me on the cameras, and they go in and change my triage notes to make me look unsafe. They keep telling me, \"Oh, it's just routine paperwork, it's a standard audit,\" but I know what they're up to. \n\nThe software we use for the reports is this really old system, anyway. It takes forever to boot up on the Toughbooks. We have these Panasonic Toughbooks in the rigs, and they're supposed to be indestructible, right? Like, you can drop them right on the pavement. I actually saw a guy drop one once right in the middle of an intersection. It was raining, too. The rain here gets so heavy in the spring, it just washes all the oil up from the asphalt and the roads get super slick. You really have to watch your following distance when it's like that. I always leave at least four car lengths, especially if I'm driving my personal car. It's a Honda, older model, but the transmission is starting to slip a little bit when it shifts from second to third. I probably need to check the transmission fluid, but you have to check it when the engine is warm, not cold. A lot of people don't know that. They check it cold and get a false reading on the dipstick, and then they overfill it. Overfilling is almost as bad as running it dry because it foams up...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like someone is putting thoughts into your mind or that events or objects around you have special meanings specifically for you?\n\nPatient: No, no one's putting thoughts in my head. That sounds like something out of a sci-fi movie, you know? Like those old black-and-white ones where they wear the tinfoil hats. I actually watched this documentary about how aluminum foil used to be made of tin, but they switched to aluminum after World War II because it was cheaper. Everything got cheaper after the war. Mass production, plastics, all that. We use so much plastic on the rig now. The IV tubing, the flushes, the sterile packaging. It all just gets thrown in the red biohazard bags. Those bags are incredibly thick, though, so they don't puncture. You don't want a needle sticking through and getting you. We have these sharps shuttles, they're little yellow plastic boxes you can carry in your pocket. I always keep mine in my left cargo pocket. The uniform pants have these great pockets with Velcro closures, but the Velcro wears out after a few months of washing them. I try to wash them on the gentle cycle, but the blood and the grease from the road, you really have to use heavy-duty detergent to get it out. I buy the big tubs of powder detergent at Costco. You can get like a hundred loads out of one tub, and it's way cheaper than the liquid stuff. Plus, the liquid stuff comes in those heavy plastic jugs that are terrible for the environment. I try to recycle them, but our city's recycling program is a mess. They only pick up every other week, and half the time they leave the bins sitting out in the middle of the street...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that certain events or objects in your environment were specifically meant for you, like they were sending you a message or had a hidden meaning just for you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. Like secret messages? No, nothing like that. I mean, the only messages I get are the dispatch tones on the pager, and those are definitely not secret. They're so loud. The tones we use are these high-pitched dual-frequency things, and they'll wake you out of a dead sleep at 3 AM. It's terrible for your heart rate, honestly. They did a study on first responders and found our resting heart rates spike like crazy when the tones drop. It's all that adrenaline. Adrenaline is a hormone, right? Produced by the adrenal glands, which sit right on top of your kidneys. Kidneys are fascinating, they filter all your blood. People who need dialysis have to go to the clinic like three times a week because their kidneys don't work. We transport a lot of dialysis patients. Some of them are really nice, but some get so cranky. I guess I'd be cranky too if I had to sit in a chair for four hours hooked up to a machine. The machines are so loud, they just beep constantly. I hate things that beep. My microwave at home beeps five times when it's done, and it won't stop until you open the door. It's a GE microwave, stainless steel. I got it on sale at Home Depot last year when they were doing their Memorial Day appliance event. They always have good sales on Memorial Day, but the parking lot is a total nightmare...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that these changes to your triage notes are part of a larger plan or conspiracy against you, or do you think this is happening randomly?\n\nPatient: It's definitely a plan. The injury review people and some of the supervisors, they're the ones watching me through the station cameras. They're changing my triage notes on purpose to make me look unsafe, even though they keep telling me it's just routine paperwork. Paperwork is such a hassle anyway. We used to do everything on actual paper, with those carbon copies where you have to press really hard with the pen to get it to go through to the yellow and pink sheets. The pink sheet always went to the hospital, and the yellow one stayed with us. I used to use these Pilot G2 pens, the 0.7 millimeter ones, because they write so smooth, but the ink smears if you're not careful. Especially if you're writing outside and it's humid. The humidity here in the summer is just brutal, it feels like you're breathing underwater. I went to Florida once for vacation, to Orlando, and it was even worse there. We went to Disney World, but the lines were so long we barely got to ride anything. Space Mountain was closed for maintenance the whole time we were there, which was a huge disappointment because I love roller coasters. The physics of roller coasters are actually really cool, how they use potential energy at the top of the hill and convert it to kinetic energy...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{
  "candidates": ["F23.80", "F31.81", "F42.2"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

I used to use these Pilot G2 pens, the 0.7 millimeter ones, because they write so smooth, but the ink smears if you're not careful. Especially if you're writing outside and it's humid. The humidity here in the summer is just brutal, it feels like you're breathing underwater. I went to Florida once for vacation, to Orlando, and it was even worse there. We went to Disney World, but the lines were so long we barely got to ride anything. Space Mountain was closed for maintenance the whole time we were there, which was a huge disappointment because I love roller coasters. The physics of roller coasters are actually really cool, how they use potential energy at the top of the hill and convert it to kinetic energy...\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like certain things that happen, like events or objects, are specifically related to you or have a hidden meaning that only you understand?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I actually watched this documentary about how aluminum foil used to be made of tin, but they switched to aluminum after World War II because it was cheaper. Everything got cheaper after the war. Mass production, plastics, all that. We use so much plastic on the rig now. The IV tubing, the flushes, the sterile packaging. It all just gets thrown in the red biohazard bags. I try to recycle them, but our city's recycling program is a mess. They only pick up every other week, and half the time they leave the bins sitting out in the middle of the street...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that certain events or objects in your environment were specifically meant for you, like they were sending you a message or had a hidden meaning just for you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. Like secret messages? No, nothing like that. I mean, the only messages I get are the dispatch tones on the pager, and those are definitely not secret. They're so loud. The tones we use are these high-pitched dual-frequency things, and they'll wake you out of a dead sleep at 3 AM. It's terrible for your heart rate, honestly. They did a study on first responders and found our resting heart rates spike like crazy when the tones drop. It's all that adrenaline. Adrenaline is a hormone, right? Produced by the adrenal glands, which sit right on top of your kidneys. Kidneys are fascinating, they filter all your blood. People who need dialysis have to go to the clinic like three times a week because their kidneys don't work. We transport a lot of dialysis patients. Some of them are really nice, but some get so cranky. I guess I'd be cranky too if I had to sit in a chair for four hours hooked up to a machine. The machines are so loud, they just beep constantly. I hate things that beep. My microwave at home beeps five times when it's done, and it won't stop until you open the door. It's a GE microwave, stainless steel. I got it on sale at Home Depot last year when they were doing their Memorial Day appliance event. They always have good sales on Memorial Day, but the parking lot is a total nightmare...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that these changes to your triage notes are part of a larger plan or conspiracy against you, or do you think this is happening randomly?\n\nPatient: It's definitely a plan. The pink sheet always went to the hospital, and the yellow one stayed with us. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hidden meanings? No, I don't... I don't really look for hidden meanings in stuff. Things just mean what they mean, you know? Like road signs. A stop sign just means stop, there's no secret message there. Although half the drivers out there act like it's a suggestion anyway. Especially over on Route 9, people just blow right through the intersections. We respond to so many T-bone collisions over there. It's always the same thing, someone trying to beat a yellow light. Yellow lights actually have a specific timing based on the speed limit of the road, I read about that once. The traffic engineers calculate the stopping distance, which involves a lot of math and physics. Friction coefficients and all that. My tires on my personal truck are getting pretty bald, honestly, so my stopping distance is probably terrible right now. I need to take it to Discount Tire, they do free rotations if you buy the tires there. I usually get the Michelin ones because they last longer, even though they cost way more up front. It's like buying cheap boots for work, you end up spending more in the long run because you have to replace them every six months. We have to wear steel-toe boots on the rig, and they get so heavy after a twelve-hour shift. My lower back just kills me by the end of the day, but I got these gel insoles that are supposed to help with the shock absorption...

I kept moving the airway bags into a pattern because I just knew it was right, you know? And I was repeating the dispatch numbers out loud. Dispatch numbers are weird now, they upgraded the CAD system last month and the screens in the rigs are way too bright. Gives you a headache if you stare at it on the night shift. I usually work nights, it's quieter, except when the bars close down on 5th street and everyone floods the diner... \n\nAnyway, I wouldn't step away from the bags. The lieutenant told me to stop, but I couldn't. It felt like the whole bay would go bad if I broke the sequence. Did you believe something specific would happen if you did?\n\nPatient: I don't really... I don't know what exactly would happen. Just bad. I didn't have a specific picture in my head or anything, I just knew I couldn't stop. It's just a feeling. Like if you don't secure the oxygen tanks right in the back of the rig. You ever hear an O2 tank hit the metal floor? It's so loud. The valves on those things are brass. \n\nBrass is getting really expensive lately, actually. I was at the hardware store on Sunday looking for a new fitting for my garden hose, and they had all the brass stuff locked behind this glass case. You have to press a little red button to get an employee to come open it. Took the guy twenty minutes to show up. He was wearing this bright orange vest... exact same color as the traffic cones we use. We lose so many cones on the interstate because people just drive right over them. They don't even slow down. The reflective tape peels off them after a week anyway...\n\nDoctor: When you were arranging the airway bags, did you have any particular thought, urge, or image that kept coming into your mind that made you feel like you had to keep doing it?\n\nPatient: No, no specific thought. I wasn't... there was no image or anything like that. My head was just kind of empty, honestly. I just had my hands on the bags. The blue ones. They use this really stiff nylon for the blue ones now, you know? The zippers always catch on the corners if you pull them too fast. We had a guy, a rookie named Miller, who ripped the zipper right off the pediatric bag last month. Just yanked it. \n\nYou can't just yank things, you have to be smooth. Like when you're driving the rig, you can't slam on the brakes or you throw everyone in the back against the bulkhead. The bulkhead in our new rig is padded, which is nice, but it smells like... I don't know, industrial glue. It's like that new car smell but way more chemical. My dad used to work at a chemical plant down by the river. They made fertilizer, I think. The river always smelled terrible in the summer when the water levels dropped. We used to go fishing down there anyway, catching catfish. Catfish will eat basically anything, they just scrape the bottom...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts were disconnected from reality or that things around you didn’t feel completely real?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, reality is just what's happening, right? Sometimes things just happen and you're in the middle of it. Like when we get a call for a multi-vehicle pileup on I-95. That feels pretty real. You get there and there's glass everywhere. Safety glass, you know, it shatters into those little cubes so it doesn't slice you up as bad as regular glass. They temper it. \n\nI actually tried to temper some steel in my garage once. I watched a bunch of videos on it. You have to heat it up until it's non-magnetic, like a cherry red color, and then quench it in oil. I used peanut oil because it has a higher smoke point, but it still smoked up the whole garage. My neighbor came over because he thought my house was on fire. He's this retired postal worker, always wearing those blue shorts they issue them, even in November. November is when the leaves finally drop off the oak tree in my front yard. I have to rake them every weekend or they kill the grass underneath. I tried that weed-and-feed stuff last spring, but I think I put too much down because it burned a huge yellow patch right near the driveway...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or ideas that other people around you didn't share, even if they tried to tell you otherwise?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... the supervisors tell me it's just routine paperwork, but I know what's going on. The injury review people and some of the supervisors, they've been watching me through the station cameras. They go in and change my triage notes to make me look unsafe. They keep saying they aren't, that it's just normal audits, but I know they are. \n\nThey have those new dome cameras in the bays now. They installed them back in March, I think. Said it was for security because someone stole a monitor out of an unlocked rig in district four. District four is mostly downtown, lots of narrow streets. It's a nightmare trying to back the rig up down there, especially when people park on both sides of the street. You have to rely on your partner to spot you, and if they aren't paying attention, you're taking off a mirror. The mirrors on the new F-series chassis are massive. They stick out like two feet. The glass on them has that little convex bubble in the corner so you can see your blind spot, but it distorts everything. It makes the cars look way farther away than they actually are. I almost backed into a fire hydrant once because of that distortion. Hydrants are supposed to be bright yellow or red, but the city painted some of them this weird dark green to blend in with the landscaping. It makes no sense. If there's a fire, you want to find the water fast, not go hunting for it in the bushes...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you know they are changing your triage notes? Have you seen them do this, or do you believe they are doing it based on something else?\n\nPatient: I just... I haven't caught them physically typing at my desk, if that's what you mean. But I know they're doing it. They watch me on the cameras, and they go in and change my triage notes to make me look unsafe. They keep telling me, \"Oh, it's just routine paperwork, it's a standard audit,\" but I know what they're up to. \n\nThe software we use for the reports is this really old system, anyway. It takes forever to boot up on the Toughbooks. We have these Panasonic Toughbooks in the rigs, and they're supposed to be indestructible, right? Like, you can drop them right on the pavement. I actually saw a guy drop one once right in the middle of an intersection. It was raining, too. The rain here gets so heavy in the spring, it just washes all the oil up from the asphalt and the roads get super slick. You really have to watch your following distance when it's like that. I always leave at least four car lengths, especially if I'm driving my personal car. It's a Honda, older model, but the transmission is starting to slip a little bit when it shifts from second to third. I probably need to check the transmission fluid, but you have to check it when the engine is warm, not cold. A lot of people don't know that. They check it cold and get a false reading on the dipstick, and then they overfill it. Overfilling is almost as bad as running it dry because it foams up...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like someone is putting thoughts into your mind or that events or objects around you have special meanings specifically for you?\n\nPatient: No, no one's putting thoughts in my head. You have to rely on your partner to spot you, and if they aren't paying attention, you're taking off a mirror. The mirrors on the new F-series chassis are massive. They stick out like two feet. The glass on them has that little convex bubble in the corner so you can see your blind spot, but it distorts everything. It makes the cars look way farther away than they actually are. I almost backed into a fire hydrant once because of that distortion. Hydrants are supposed to be bright yellow or red, but the city painted some of them this weird dark green to blend in with the landscaping. It makes no sense. If there's a fire, you want to find the water fast, not go hunting for it in the bushes...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you know they are changing your triage notes? Have you seen them do this, or do you believe they are doing it based on something else?\n\nPatient: I just... I haven't caught them physically typing at my desk, if that's what you mean. But I know they're doing it. They watch me on the cameras, and they go in and change my triage notes to make me look unsafe. They keep telling me, \"Oh, it's just routine paperwork, it's a standard audit,\" but I know what they're up to. \n\nThe software we use for the reports is this really old system, anyway. It takes forever to boot up on the Toughbooks. We have these Panasonic Toughbooks in the rigs, and they're supposed to be indestructible, right? Like, you can drop them right on the pavement. I actually saw a guy drop one once right in the middle of an intersection. It was raining, too. The rain here gets so heavy in the spring, it just washes all the oil up from the asphalt and the roads get super slick. You really have to watch your following distance when it's like that. I always leave at least four car lengths, especially if I'm driving my personal car. It's a Honda, older model, but the transmission is starting to slip a little bit when it shifts from second to third. I probably need to check the transmission fluid, but you have to check it when the engine is warm, not cold. A lot of people don't know that. They check it cold and get a false reading on the dipstick, and then they overfill it. Overfilling is almost as bad as running it dry because it foams up...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like someone is putting thoughts into your mind or that events or objects around you have special meanings specifically for you?\n\nPatient: No, no one's putting thoughts in my head. That sounds like something out of a sci-fi movie, you know? Like those old black-and-white ones where they wear the tinfoil hats. I actually watched this documentary about how aluminum foil used to be made of tin, but they switched to aluminum after World War II because it was cheaper. Everything got cheaper after the war. Mass production, plastics, all that. We use so much plastic on the rig now. The IV tubing, the flushes, the sterile packaging. It all just gets thrown in the red biohazard bags. Those bags are incredibly thick, though, so they don't puncture. You don't want a needle sticking through and getting you. We have these sharps shuttles, they're little yellow plastic boxes you can carry in your pocket. I always keep mine in my left cargo pocket. The uniform pants have these great pockets with Velcro closures, but the Velcro wears out after a few months of washing them. I try to wash them on the gentle cycle, but the blood and the grease from the road, you really have to use heavy-duty detergent to get it out. I buy the big tubs of powder detergent at Costco. You can get like a hundred loads out of one tub, and it's way cheaper than the liquid stuff. Plus, the liquid stuff comes in those heavy plastic jugs that are terrible for the environment. I try to recycle them, but our city's recycling program is a mess. They only pick up every other week, and half the time they leave the bins sitting out in the middle of the street...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that certain events or objects in your environment were specifically meant for you, like they were sending you a message or had a hidden meaning just for you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. Like secret messages? No, nothing like that. I mean, the only messages I get are the dispatch tones on the pager, and those are definitely not secret. They're so loud. The tones we use are these high-pitched dual-frequency things, and they'll wake you out of a dead sleep at 3 AM. It's terrible for your heart rate, honestly. They did a study on first responders and found our resting heart rates spike like crazy when the tones drop. It's all that adrenaline. Adrenaline is a hormone, right? Produced by the adrenal glands, which sit right on top of your kidneys. Kidneys are fascinating, they filter all your blood. People who need dialysis have to go to the clinic like three times a week because their kidneys don't work. We transport a lot of dialysis patients. Some of them are really nice, but some get so cranky. I guess I'd be cranky too if I had to sit in a chair for four hours hooked up to a machine. The machines are so loud, they just beep constantly. I hate things that beep. My microwave at home beeps five times when it's done, and it won't stop until you open the door. It's a GE microwave, stainless steel. I got it on sale at Home Depot last year when they were doing their Memorial Day appliance event. They always have good sales on Memorial Day, but the parking lot is a total nightmare...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that these changes to your triage notes are part of a larger plan or conspiracy against you, or do you think this is happening randomly?\n\nPatient: It's definitely a plan. The injury review people and some of the supervisors, they're the ones watching me through the station cameras. They're changing my triage notes on purpose to make me look unsafe, even though they keep telling me it's just routine paperwork. Paperwork is such a hassle anyway. We used to do everything on actual paper, with those carbon copies where you have to press really hard with the pen to get it to go through to the yellow and pink sheets. The pink sheet always went to the hospital, and the yellow one stayed with us. I used to use these Pilot G2 pens, the 0.7 millimeter ones, because they write so smooth, but the ink smears if you're not careful. Especially if you're writing outside and it's humid. The humidity here in the summer is just brutal, it feels like you're breathing underwater. I went to Florida once for vacation, to Orlando, and it was even worse there. We went to Disney World, but the lines were so long we barely got to ride anything. Space Mountain was closed for maintenance the whole time we were there, which was a huge disappointment because I love roller coasters. The physics of roller coasters are actually really cool, how they use potential energy at the top of the hill and convert it to kinetic energy...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like certain things that happen, like events or objects, are specifically related to you or have a hidden meaning that only you understand?\n\nPatient: Hidden meanings? No, I don't... I don't really look for hidden meanings in stuff. Things just mean what they mean, you know? Like road signs. A stop sign just means stop, there's no secret message there. Although half the drivers out there act like it's a suggestion anyway. Especially over on Route 9, people just blow right through the intersections. We respond to so many T-bone collisions over there. It's always the same thing, someone trying to beat a yellow light. Yellow lights actually have a specific timing based on the speed limit of the road, I read about that once. The traffic engineers calculate the stopping distance, which involves a lot of math and physics. Friction coefficients and all that. My tires on my personal truck are getting pretty bald, honestly, so my stopping distance is probably terrible right now. I need to take it to Discount Tire, they do free rotations if you buy the tires there. I usually get the Michelin ones because they last longer, even though they cost way more up front. It's like buying cheap boots for work, you end up spending more in the long run because you have to replace them every six months. We have to wear steel-toe boots on the rig, and they get so heavy after a twelve-hour shift. My lower back just kills me by the end of the day, but I got these gel insoles that are supposed to help with the shock absorption...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I'm not... I'm not involved in anything bigger, I don't have a special role or anything like that. I'm just an EMT. I just want to do my job, but they're trying to make me look bad with the notes. I don't know why they picked me. Maybe they just need someone to blame when their numbers look bad. The metrics they track us on are ridiculous anyway. Chute times, scene times, turnaround times at the hospital. They want us back in service within twenty minutes of dropping off a patient, but half the time we're stuck in the ER hallway waiting for a bed because the hospital is on diversion. Diversion is when they tell dispatch they can't take any more ambulances because they're full. But if every hospital in the county goes on diversion, dispatch just overrides it and we have to bring them anyway. It's called being on "force open." The charge nurses get so mad when we walk through the doors during force open. You can see the steam coming out of their ears. I try to bring them donuts sometimes to smooth things over. There's this little bakery near station four that makes these amazing apple fritters, they're like the size of your head. The guy who runs it gets there at 2 AM every day to start baking. I don't know how people function on a schedule like that long-term, waking up that early. I mean, shift work is hard enough on your circadian rhythm. Melatonin only does so much, and if you take too much you get those weird, vivid dreams...
